Good — the wrong property result is fixed, but this output shows the next issue:

"intent": "general",
"results": [],
"semantic_results": []

For a general knowledge question, your API should not say “I couldn't find any properties”. It should return the relevant Chroma knowledge result.

The likely problem is in hybrid_search.py: your semantic search is probably still being skipped because has_structured_filters is True due to country="Oman".
1. Check has_structured_filters

Run:

grep -n "has_structured_filters" -A15 -B10 app/services/hybrid_search.py

You want it to look like:

understood = understand_query(query)

intent = understood.get("intent")

has_structured_filters = (
    intent == "property_search"
    and any(
        [
            brand,
            country,
            city,
            property_type,
        ]
    )
)

The critical part is:

intent == "property_search"

Therefore:

What are the Oman Golden Visa requirements?

has:

intent = general
country = Oman

and must produce:

has_structured_filters = False

2. Make sure semantic search runs

Your section should be:

# --------------------------------------------------------
# Semantic search
# --------------------------------------------------------
if has_structured_filters:
    semantic_results = []
else:
    semantic_filters = {}

    if intent == "property_search":
        semantic_filters["document_type"] = "property"

    semantic_results = _chroma_results(
        query,
        n_results=limit,
        filters=semantic_filters,
    )

For the Golden Visa query, this means:

semantic_filters = {}

So Chroma can search the category document containing the Golden Visa information.
3. There is one more problem in app/api/chat.py

Your build_message() currently does this:

if not results:
    return (
        "I couldn't find any properties matching "
        f'your query: "{query}".'
    )

But results means structured property results only.

For a general question, that's wrong.

Change the function signature from:

def build_message(
    query: str,
    understood: dict,
    results: list[dict],
) -> str:

to:

def build_message(
    query: str,
    understood: dict,
    results: list[dict],
    semantic_results: list[dict],
) -> str:

Then change the beginning to:

if not results and not semantic_results:
    return (
        "I couldn't find any information matching "
        f'your query: "{query}".'
    )

if not results and semantic_results:
    return (
        "I found relevant information for your query."
    )

And when calling it:

message = build_message(
    query=query,
    understood=understood,
    results=structured_results,
    semantic_results=semantic_results,
)

4. But don't stop there

Your current API response exposes:

"semantic_results": [
    {
        "document": "...Golden Visa..."
    }
]

That's useful for debugging, but the message should eventually actually answer the question, not just say:

I found relevant information for your query.

For now, though, the immediate goal should be to get:

curl -X POST http://127.0.0.1:8000/api/chat \

to return something like:

  },

      "document": "...Oman Golden Visa..."
    }

Then we can make the final response actually summarize the relevant Chroma content.

Your database/ingestion is already doing the important part correctly. The next step is connecting the semantic result into the response message.

Perfect. We found the exact problem.
What is happening

Chroma is finding the correct Oman category document:

distance = 1.0634740591049194
document_type = category

But _chroma_results() returns 0 because your distance filtering is rejecting it.

Your chroma_store.py currently has:

distance_threshold: float = 1.2

so Chroma itself would accept 1.063.

But hybrid_search.py has another threshold:

if distance is not None and distance > MIN_SEMANTIC_DISTANCE:
    continue

Your MIN_SEMANTIC_DISTANCE is almost certainly below 1.063.
1. Check the value

Run:

grep -n "MIN_SEMANTIC_DISTANCE" -B3 -A3 app/services/hybrid_search.py

I expect you'll see something like:

MIN_SEMANTIC_DISTANCE = 0.8

or similar.
2. Recommended fix

Since Chroma is returning:

1.0634740591049194

for the relevant Golden Visa document, increase the threshold.

In:

app/services/hybrid_search.py

find:

MIN_SEMANTIC_DISTANCE = ...

and change it to:

MIN_SEMANTIC_DISTANCE = 1.2

For example:

# Maximum Chroma distance accepted for semantic matches.
MIN_SEMANTIC_DISTANCE = 1.2

Why 1.2?

Your chroma_store.py already uses:

distance_threshold: float = 1.2

So having both layers use the same threshold keeps the behavior consistent.

        document_type = semantic_item.get(
            "document_type",

def hybrid_search(
    db: Session,
    *,
    query: str,
    filters: dict[str, Any] | None = None,
    brand: str | None = None,
    country: str | None = None,
    city: str | None = None,
    property_type: str | None = None,
    limit: int = 5,
) -> dict[str, Any]:
    """
    Hybrid retrieval pipeline.

    Structured property records are always the primary source
    for property searches.

    Chroma provides semantic supporting evidence.

    Semantic documents are never presented as actual
    Property records.
    """

    filters = filters or {}

    # --------------------------------------------------------
    # Resolve filters.
    #
    # The API normally passes the complete "understood"
    # dictionary. Explicit keyword arguments remain supported
    # for compatibility.
    # --------------------------------------------------------

    brand = (
        brand
        if brand is not None
        else filters.get("brand")
    )

    country = (
        country
        if country is not None
        else filters.get("country")
    )

    city = (
        city
        if city is not None
        else filters.get("city")
    )

    property_type = (
        property_type
        if property_type is not None
        else filters.get("property_type")
    )

    property_type = normalize_property_type(
        property_type
    )

    intent = filters.get("intent")

    # --------------------------------------------------------
    # Structured search
    # --------------------------------------------------------
    if intent == "property_search":
        structured_objects = structured_search(
            db,
            brand=brand,
            country=(
                country
                if country is not None
                else filters.get("country")
            ),
            city=city,
            property_type=property_type,
            limit=limit,
        )
    else:
        structured_objects = []

    has_structured_filters = any(
        [
            brand,
            country,
            city,
            property_type,
        ]
    )

    structured_results = [
        _property_to_dict(item)
        for item in structured_objects
    ]

    # --------------------------------------------------------
    # Semantic search
    # --------------------------------------------------------
    if has_structured_filters:
        semantic_results = []
    else:
        semantic_filters = {
            key: value
            for key, value in {
                "document_type": (
                    "property"
                    if intent == "property_search"
                    else None
                ),
                "country": country,
                "city": city,
                "brand": brand,
                "property_type": (
                    normalize_property_type(property_type)
                    if property_type
                    else None
                ),
            }.items()
            if value
        }
        if intent == "property_search":
            semantic_filters["document_type"] = "property"

        semantic_results = _chroma_results(
(.venv) vishnu@netops-Precision-3490:~/real-estate-ai-poc/backend$ sed -n '1,120p' app/services/hybrid_search.py
from typing import Any

from sqlalchemy.orm import Session

from app.models.property import Property
from app.knowledge.chroma_store import search as chroma_search
from app.services.query_understanding import understand_query


MIN_SEMANTIC_DISTANCE = 1.2


PROPERTY_TYPE_ALIASES = {
    "residence": "hotel_residences",
    "residences": "hotel_residences",
    "hotel residence": "hotel_residences",
    "hotel residences": "hotel_residences",
    "branded residence": "hotel_residences",
    "branded residences": "hotel_residences",

    "apartment": "apartments",
    "apartments": "apartments",

    "villa": "villas",
    "villas": "villas",

    "hotel resort": "hotel_resort",
    "hotel & resort": "hotel_resort",
    "hotel and resort": "hotel_resort",
    "resort": "hotel_resort",
}


def normalize_property_type(
    value: str | None,
) -> str | None:
    """
    Convert user-facing property type aliases into the
    canonical values used by the database.
    """

    if not value:
        return None

    normalized = value.strip().lower()

    return PROPERTY_TYPE_ALIASES.get(
        normalized,
        value,
    )


def structured_search(
    db: Session,
    *,
    brand: str | None = None,
    country: str | None = None,
    city: str | None = None,
    property_type: str | None = None,
    limit: int = 5,
) -> list[Property]:
    """
    Search actual Property records using structured filters.
    """

    query = db.query(Property)

    if country:
        query = query.filter(
            Property.country.ilike(
                f"%{country}%"
            )
        )

    if city:
        query = query.filter(
            Property.city.ilike(
                f"%{city}%"
            )
        )

    if property_type:
        canonical_type = normalize_property_type(
            property_type
        )

        query = query.filter(
            Property.property_type.ilike(
                f"%{canonical_type}%"
            )
        )

    if brand:
        query = query.filter(
            Property.brands.ilike(
                f"%{brand}%"
            )
        )

    return (
        query
        .order_by(Property.id.asc())
        .limit(limit)
        .all()
    )


def _property_to_dict(
    property_item: Property,
) -> dict[str, Any]:
    """
    Convert SQLAlchemy Property into API-safe dictionary.
    """

    return {
        "id": property_item.id,
        "source": property_item.source,
        "title": property_item.title,
        "project_name": property_item.project_name,
        "developer": property_item.developer,

ChatGPT said:

Your structured_search() is working correctly. The problem is now in hybrid_search() filter resolution / API call, not SQLite.

Your direct test proves:

structured_search(db, country="Oman")
→ 1 result
→ Marriott Residences Oman

But /api/chat returns zero. That means hybrid_search() is likely receiving intent incorrectly because chat.py is passing individual filters but not passing the full filters dictionary.
Change app/api/chat.py

You currently have:

retrieval = hybrid_search(
    db=db,
    query=query,
    brand=understood.get("brand"),
    country=understood.get("country"),
    city=understood.get("city"),
    property_type=understood.get("property_type"),
)

Change it to:

retrieval = hybrid_search(
    db=db,
    query=query,
    filters=understood,
    brand=understood.get("brand"),
    country=understood.get("country"),
    city=understood.get("city"),
    property_type=understood.get("property_type"),
)

Why this fixes it

Inside hybrid_search() you have:

intent = filters.get("intent")

But if filters isn't passed, this line gets:

filters = {}

so:

intent = None

Then this condition fails:

if intent == "property_search":

and structured search is skipped.

That's exactly consistent with your symptoms:

    Direct structured_search(country="Oman") → works
    /api/chat → zero results
    filters in API response correctly shows "country": "Oman" because understand_query() works

Then restart FastAPI

# stop the existing server
Ctrl+C

Then start it again, for example:

uvicorn app.main:app --reload

One more important issue

Your Golden Visa query is different:

What are the Oman Golden Visa requirements?

That's a general knowledge query, not a property search. For that one, you actually want Chroma semantic search to run.

Your current logic has:

has_structured_filters = any(
    [
        brand,
        country,
        city,
        property_type,
    ]
)

Because "Oman" is extracted, has_structured_filters becomes True, so semantic search gets disabled:

if has_structured_filters:
    semantic_results = []

That is why the Golden Visa query can still fail even after fixing chat.py.

I would make the next change to this section:

if has_structured_filters:
    semantic_results = []
else:
    ...

to:

if intent == "property_search" and has_structured_filters:
    semantic_results = []
else:
    semantic_filters = {
        key: value
        for key, value in {
            "document_type": (
                "property"
                if intent == "property_search"
                else None
            ),
            "country": country,
            "city": city,
            "brand": brand,
            "property_type": (
                normalize_property_type(property_type)
                if property_type
                else None
            ),
        }.items()
        if value
    }

    semantic_results = _chroma_results(
        query,
        n_results=limit,
        filters=semantic_filters,
    )

The key distinction is:

intent == "property_search"

For property searches, structured filters control the result.

For general questions such as Golden Visa requirements, country should not prevent semantic retrieval.
So make these two changes first

    app/api/chat.py — pass filters=understood.
    app/services/hybrid_search.py — don't disable semantic search merely because a country was extracted from a general-information question.

Then test these four again:

Show me properties in Oman
Show me properties in France
What are the Oman Golden Visa requirements?
Tell me about Marriott Residences Oman

Phase 1 — Advanced Search

This should be our immediate next step.

Current search handles:

country
city
brand
property_type

We should expand it to support things such as:

budget
min_price
max_price
bedrooms
min_area
max_area
developer
investment_type
listing_type
status
location

For example:

    Show me villas in Dubai under $2M

should become something like:

{
  "intent": "property_search",
  "country": "United Arab Emirates",
  "city": "Dubai",
  "property_type": "villas",
  "max_price": 2000000
}

Then make structured_search() understand those filters.

This is the next thing I'd implement.
Phase 2 — Location Data

Before building the Location Engine, we need actual location data.

Start with a small, reliable dataset rather than trying to build the entire world.

For each property/location:

latitude
longitude
neighborhood
city
country

Then add POIs:

schools
hospitals
shopping
restaurants
beaches
airports
metro
major roads

We can initially use public geospatial data rather than building a huge custom data platform.
Phase 3 — Location Engine

Once location data exists:

Property
   ↓
coordinates
   ↓
nearby POIs
   ↓
distance calculations
   ↓
accessibility
   ↓
Location Score

For example:

{
  "location_score": 84,
  "school_proximity_score": 82,
  "hospital_proximity_score": 91,
  "lifestyle_score": 87,
  "airport_access_score": 78
}

Most importantly, store the evidence behind every score.

Don't just return:

"location_score": 84

Return:

{
  "score": 84,
  "evidence": [
    "... km from airport",
    "... km from hospital",
    "... km from schools"
  ]
}

That becomes important later for the AI and guardrails.
Phase 4 — Infrastructure Engine

Then calculate things such as:

Road access
Metro/transit
Airport access
Hospitals
Schools
Major developments
Future infrastructure

Output:

{
  "infrastructure_score": 79,
  "transport_score": 83,
  "road_access_score": 81,
  "airport_access_score": 72
}

Again: evidence + source + timestamp.
Phase 5 — Comparable Engine

Now we have enough information to compare properties.

For example:

Target property
      ↓
Find similar properties
      ↓
Location similarity
Property type
Bedrooms
Area
Price
Developer
      ↓
Comparable Score

Output:

  "comparables": [...],

  "comparable_price_per_sqft": 1720,
  "comparable_confidence": 0.82
}

This becomes the foundation of valuation.
Phase 6 — Valuation Engine

Then:

Comparable Engine
       +
Property attributes
       ↓
Valuation

For example:

{

  "fair_value_low": 1750000,

  "asking_price": 1650000,
  "potential_upside": 0.121,
  "valuation_confidence": 0.78
}

Important: keep this as indicative analysis, not a guaranteed valuation.
Phase 7 — Growth Engine

Then analyze:

Location
Infrastructure
Development activity
Market signals
Comparable trends

and produce:

{

}

Phase 8 — Risk Engine

Then combine all uncertainty.

For example:

Data quality
Comparable confidence
Valuation uncertainty
Market risk
Infrastructure risk

Output:

{
  "risk_score": 31,
  "risk_level": "medium",
  "confidence": 0.78,
  "risks": [
    "...",
    "..."
  ]
}

I'd actually define this carefully so that higher score = better/lower risk or higher score = more risk, and use one convention everywhere. Don't mix them.
Phase 9 — Investment Profile

Then introduce the investor.

Example:

{
  "budget": 2000000,
  "risk_tolerance": "medium",
  "investment_horizon": "5-10 years",
  "objective": "capital_appreciation",

    "Dubai",
    "Muscat"
  ],
  "preferred_property_type": "villas"
}

This should be persisted in SQLite.
Phase 10 — Personalization

Now the same property can score differently for different investors.

Example:

Property A
Base Deal Score = 82

Investor 1:
Capital appreciation
Low risk
Long horizon

→ Personalized Score = 87

Investor 2:
Rental income
Short horizon

→ Personalized Score = 71

This is where your system starts becoming more than a normal property-search application.
Phase 11 — Deal Engine

Then combine everything:

Location
Infrastructure
Comparables
Valuation
Growth
Risk
Investor profile
       ↓
Personalized Deal Score

For example:

{
  "deal_score": 88,
  "location_score": 84,
  "infrastructure_score": 81,
  "comparable_score": 86,
  "valuation_score": 91,
  "growth_score": 89,
  "risk_score": 79,
  "confidence": 0.83
}

And, crucially:

WHY?
 ├── Evidence
 ├── Positive factors
 ├── Risks
 └── Confidence

Phase 12 — AI Orchestration

Only after those engines work independently should we make the LLM orchestrate them.

For example:

    Which property is the best investment for me in Dubai?

The orchestrator should determine:

Intent
   ↓
User profile
   ↓
Advanced search
   ↓
Comparables
   ↓
Valuation
   ↓
Growth
   ↓
Risk
   ↓
Deal ranking
   ↓
Evidence
   ↓
LLM explanation

The LLM should explain the computed intelligence, not invent the numbers.
Phase 13 — Guardrails

Then add:
Pre-LLM

Input validation

PII protection
Query scope
Data freshness
Evidence availability

Post-LLM

Schema validation
Numeric validation

Hallucination detection
Risk disclosure
Financial boundaries

This becomes especially important once valuation and investment recommendations are involved.

def _chroma_results(
    query: str,
    n_results: int = 5,
    filters: dict[str, Any] | None = None,
) -> list[dict[str, Any]]:
    """
    Retrieve semantically similar documents from Chroma.
    """

    if not query.strip():
        return []

    try:
        results = chroma_search(
            query=query,
            n_results=n_results,
            filters=filters,
        )
    except Exception:
        return []

    documents = (
        results.get("documents") or [[]]
    )[0]

    metadatas = (
        results.get("metadatas") or [[]]
    )[0]

    distances = (
        results.get("distances") or [[]]
    )[0]

    output = []

    for index, document in enumerate(documents):

        metadata = (
            metadatas[index]
            if index < len(metadatas)
            else {}
        )

        distance = (
            distances[index]
            if index < len(distances)
            else None
        )

        if (
            distance is not None
            and distance > MIN_SEMANTIC_DISTANCE
        ):
            continue

        document_type = (
            metadata.get("document_type")
            or "general"
        )

        output.append(
            {
                "document": document,
                "metadata": metadata,
                "distance": distance,
                "document_type": document_type,
            }
        )

    return output


def _is_same_property(
    semantic_item: dict[str, Any],
    structured_item: dict[str, Any],
) -> bool:
    """
    Determine whether a semantic document represents the
    same property already returned by SQL.
    """

    metadata = semantic_item.get(
        "metadata",
        {},
    )

    semantic_url = (
        metadata.get("source_url")
        or ""
    ).strip().lower()

    structured_url = (
        structured_item.get("source_url")
        or ""
    ).strip().lower()

    if (
        semantic_url
        and structured_url
        and semantic_url == structured_url
    ):
        return True

    semantic_property = (
        metadata.get("property_name")
        or ""
    ).strip().lower()

    structured_property = (
        structured_item.get("project_name")
        or ""
    ).strip().lower()

    if (
        semantic_property
        and structured_property
        and semantic_property == structured_property
    ):
        return True

    return False


def _filter_semantic_results(
    semantic_results: list[dict[str, Any]],
    structured_results: list[dict[str, Any]],
) -> list[dict[str, Any]]:
    """
    Remove semantic property documents that are already
    represented by structured SQL results.

    Category pages and press releases remain available as
    supporting evidence.
    """

    filtered = []

    for semantic_item in semantic_results:

        document_type = semantic_item.get(
            "document_type",
            "general",
        )

        if document_type == "property":

            duplicate = any(
                _is_same_property(
                    semantic_item,
                    structured_item,
                )
                for structured_item in structured_results
            )

            if duplicate:
                continue

        filtered.append(
            semantic_item
        )

    return filtered

    db: Session,
    *,
    query: str,
    filters: dict[str, Any] | None = None,
    brand: str | None = None,
    country: str | None = None,
    city: str | None = None,
    property_type: str | None = None,
    limit: int = 5,
) -> dict[str, Any]:
    """
    Hybrid retrieval pipeline.

    Structured property records are always the primary source

Step 1 — Extend query_understanding.py

1. Add these helper functions after find_value()

def extract_number(
    text: str,
    pattern: str,
) -> float | None:
    match = re.search(pattern, text.lower())

    if not match:
        return None

    try:
        return float(
            match.group(1).replace(",", "")
        )
    except (ValueError, AttributeError):
        return None


def extract_max_price(
    text: str,
) -> float | None:
    patterns = [
        r"(?:under|below|less than|up to)\s*\$?\s*([\d,]+(?:\.\d+)?)\s*(million|m|k)?",
        r"\$?\s*([\d,]+(?:\.\d+)?)\s*(million|m|k)?\s*(?:or less|maximum|max)",
    ]

    for pattern in patterns:
        match = re.search(
            pattern,
            text.lower(),
        )

        if not match:
            continue

        try:
            value = float(
                match.group(1).replace(",", "")
            )

            unit = match.group(2)

            if unit in ("million", "m"):
                value *= 1_000_000
            elif unit == "k":
                value *= 1_000

            return value

        except (ValueError, AttributeError):
            continue

    return None


def extract_min_price(
    text: str,
) -> float | None:
    patterns = [
        r"(?:over|above|more than|from)\s*\$?\s*([\d,]+(?:\.\d+)?)\s*(million|m|k)?",
        r"\$?\s*([\d,]+(?:\.\d+)?)\s*(million|m|k)?\s*(?:or more|minimum|min)",
    ]

    for pattern in patterns:
        match = re.search(
            pattern,
            text.lower(),
        )

        if not match:
            continue

        try:
            value = float(
                match.group(1).replace(",", "")
            )

            unit = match.group(2)

            if unit in ("million", "m"):
                value *= 1_000_000
            elif unit == "k":
                value *= 1_000

            return value

        except (ValueError, AttributeError):
            continue

    return None


def extract_bedrooms(
    text: str,
) -> int | None:
    match = re.search(
        r"(\d+)\s*(?:bedroom|bedrooms|br)\b",
        text.lower(),
    )

    if not match:
        return None

    return int(match.group(1))


def extract_bathrooms(
    text: str,
) -> int | None:
    match = re.search(
        r"(\d+)\s*(?:bathroom|bathrooms|bath|baths)\b",
        text.lower(),
    )

    if not match:
        return None

    return int(match.group(1))

def structured_search(
    db: Session,
    *,
    brand: str | None = None,
    country: str | None = None,
    city: str | None = None,
    property_type: str | None = None,
    min_price: float | None = None,
    max_price: float | None = None,
    bedrooms: int | None = None,
    bathrooms: int | None = None,
    limit: int = 5,
) -> list[Property]:

Then add these filters after the existing brand filter:

    if min_price is not None:
        query = query.filter(
            Property.price >= min_price
        )

    if max_price is not None:
        query = query.filter(
            Property.price <= max_price
        )

    if bedrooms is not None:
        query = query.filter(
            Property.bedrooms == bedrooms
        )

    if bathrooms is not None:
        query = query.filter(
            Property.bathrooms == bathrooms
        )

So your structured search becomes capable of:

country
city
brand
property_type
min_price
max_price
bedrooms
bathrooms

Yes — this is a good point to move forward. Your core ingestion + normalization + structured/semantic retrieval foundation is working, and the latest tests also show that the query-understanding layer is already extracting things like country, city, property type, bedrooms, and max price.

Given your architecture, I would skip Wasalt for now and build the intelligence stack in this order:
What we've covered

    DarGlobal ingestion
        Scraping/source ingestion
        Raw content
        Source URLs
        Crawl metadata

    Normalization
        Property normalization
        Document type detection
        property, category, press_release, general
        Property type normalization
        Brand/country/city extraction

        Property model
        Actual property records
        Structured fields
        JSON fields for amenities/brands/images
        Source metadata

        Semantic document indexing
        Ollama embeddings
        Metadata
        Distance filtering
        Property/category/press-release distinction

    Query understanding
        Intent detection
        Country
        City
        Brand
        Property type
        Bedrooms
        Max price

        SQLite is primary for property searches
        Chroma is supporting semantic retrieval
        Duplicate property filtering
        Semantic threshold
        Structured filtering

    Chat API
        /api/chat
        Request/response schemas
        Natural-language property search
        General semantic questions
        Basic response generation

    Important retrieval bug fixed
        General queries were incorrectly being restricted to property documents.
        Property queries are now restricted appropriately.
        Structured property search works independently of Chroma.

So this part of the architecture is effectively:

Ingestion
   ↓
Normalization
   ↓
SQLite + ChromaDB
   ↓
Query Understanding
   ↓
Hybrid Search
   ↓
FastAPI Chat

What I recommend building next

I would not jump directly into the LLM/orchestration layer yet.

Build the intelligence engines first, because the AI orchestration layer will eventually call these engines.
Phase 1 — Advanced Search

This should be the immediate next step.

Your current structured_search() supports:

country
city
property_type
brand

But your query parser is already producing:

bedrooms
max_price

So those filters need to actually reach SQL.

For example:

Show me villas in Dubai under $2 million

currently produces:

{
  "city": "Dubai",
  "property_type": "villas",
  "max_price": 2000000
}

but structured_search() doesn't yet use max_price.

Likewise:

Show me 3 bedroom villas in Dubai

produces:

{
  "city": "Dubai",
  "property_type": "villas",
  "bedrooms": 3
}

but SQL doesn't yet filter bedrooms.
Therefore next implementation:

Advanced Search Engine
├── country
├── city
├── property_type
├── brand
├── bedrooms
├── bathrooms
├── min_price
├── max_price
├── min_area
├── max_area
├── listing_type
├── status
└── sorting

This is the highest-value next step because every later engine depends on reliable property selection.

After advanced search:

   ↓
latitude / longitude
   ↓
Location dataset

Something like:

Location
├── property_id
├── latitude
├── longitude
├── address
├── district
├── city
├── country
└── geo_source

Then collect public location information.

For example:

Property
   │
   ├── schools
   ├── hospitals
   ├── airports
   ├── shopping
   ├── restaurants
   ├── beaches
   ├── parks
   └── transit

This becomes the foundation for the Location Intelligence Engine.
Phase 3 — Location Intelligence Engine

distance to nearest school

distance to beach

Location Score: 87/100

Capital appreciation investor

Sources

This is the point where your system starts delivering real estate intelligence, rather than just search.

AI Orchestration

1. Update structured_search() in app/services/hybrid_search.py

Replace your current structured_search() with this version:
Advanced structured_search implementation

def structured_search(
    db: Session,
    *,
    brand: str | None = None,
    country: str | None = None,
    city: str | None = None,
    property_type: str | None = None,
    bedrooms: int | None = None,
    bathrooms: int | None = None,
    min_price: float | None = None,
    max_price: float | None = None,
    min_area: float | None = None,
    max_area: float | None = None,
    listing_type: str | None = None,
    status: str | None = None,
    limit: int = 5,
) -> list[Property]:
    """
    Search actual Property records using structured filters.
    """

    query = db.query(Property)

    # --------------------------------------------------------
    # Location
    # --------------------------------------------------------

    if country:
        query = query.filter(
            Property.country.ilike(
                f"%{country}%"
            )
        )

    if city:
        query = query.filter(
            Property.city.ilike(
                f"%{city}%"
            )
        )

    # --------------------------------------------------------
    # Property
    # --------------------------------------------------------

    if property_type:
        canonical_type = normalize_property_type(
            property_type
        )

        query = query.filter(
            Property.property_type.ilike(
                f"%{canonical_type}%"
            )
        )

    if brand:
        query = query.filter(
            Property.brands.ilike(
                f"%{brand}%"
            )
        )

    # --------------------------------------------------------
    # Bedrooms / bathrooms
    # --------------------------------------------------------

    if bedrooms is not None:
        query = query.filter(
            Property.bedrooms == bedrooms
        )

    if bathrooms is not None:
        query = query.filter(
            Property.bathrooms >= bathrooms
        )

    # --------------------------------------------------------
    # Price
    # --------------------------------------------------------

    if min_price is not None:
        query = query.filter(
            Property.price >= min_price
        )

    if max_price is not None:
        query = query.filter(
            Property.price <= max_price
        )

    # --------------------------------------------------------
    # Area
    # --------------------------------------------------------

    if min_area is not None:
        query = query.filter(
            Property.area >= min_area
        )

    if max_area is not None:
        query = query.filter(
            Property.area <= max_area
        )

    # --------------------------------------------------------
    # Listing / status
    # --------------------------------------------------------

    if listing_type:
        query = query.filter(
            Property.listing_type.ilike(
                f"%{listing_type}%"
            )
        )

    if status:
        query = query.filter(
            Property.status.ilike(
                f"%{status}%"
            )
        )

    return (
        query
        .order_by(Property.id.asc())
        .limit(limit)
        .all()
    )

from app.db.database import SessionLocal
from app.services.hybrid_search import structured_search

db = SessionLocal()

tests = [
    {
        "name": "Oman",
        "country": "Oman",
    },
    {
        "name": "Dubai villas",
        "city": "Dubai",
        "property_type": "villas",
    },
    {
        "name": "Dubai 3BR villas",
        "city": "Dubai",
        "property_type": "villas",
        "bedrooms": 3,
    },
    {
        "name": "Under 2M",
        "max_price": 2000000,
    },
]

for test in tests:
    name = test.pop("name")

    results = structured_search(
        db,
        **test,
        limit=5,
    )

    print("\nTEST:", name)
    print("RESULT COUNT:", len(results))

    for p in results:
        print(
            p.id,
            p.project_name,
            p.country,
            p.city,
            p.property_type,
            p.bedrooms,
            p.price,
        )

db.close()
'

Absolutely. Let's keep the first version of the Location model simple and extensible. It should store the canonical geographic location of each property without mixing in POIs or scoring yet.
1. Create app/models/location.py
Property Location model

from datetime import datetime

from sqlalchemy import DateTime, Float, ForeignKey, Integer, String
from sqlalchemy.orm import Mapped, mapped_column, relationship

from app.db.database import Base


class PropertyLocation(Base):
    __tablename__ = "property_locations"

    id: Mapped[int] = mapped_column(
        Integer,
        primary_key=True,
        autoincrement=True,
    )

    property_id: Mapped[int] = mapped_column(
        ForeignKey("properties.id"),
        unique=True,
        index=True,
    )

    # --------------------------------------------------------
    # Geographic coordinates
    # --------------------------------------------------------

    latitude: Mapped[float | None] = mapped_column(
        Float,
        nullable=True,
    )

    longitude: Mapped[float | None] = mapped_column(
        Float,
        nullable=True,
    )

    # --------------------------------------------------------
    # Address information
    # --------------------------------------------------------

    address: Mapped[str | None] = mapped_column(
        String(1000),
        nullable=True,
    )

    district: Mapped[str | None] = mapped_column(
        String(255),
        nullable=True,
    )

    city: Mapped[str | None] = mapped_column(
        String(150),
        index=True,
        nullable=True,
    )

    country: Mapped[str | None] = mapped_column(
        String(100),
        index=True,
        nullable=True,
    )

    # --------------------------------------------------------
    # Data source
    # --------------------------------------------------------

    source: Mapped[str | None] = mapped_column(
        String(100),
        nullable=True,
    )

    source_url: Mapped[str | None] = mapped_column(
        String(1000),
        nullable=True,
    )

    # --------------------------------------------------------
    # Geocoding metadata
    # --------------------------------------------------------

    geocoded_at: Mapped[datetime | None] = mapped_column(
        DateTime,
        nullable=True,
    )

    # --------------------------------------------------------
    # Relationship
    # --------------------------------------------------------

    property: Mapped["Property"] = relationship(
        back_populates="location_data",
    )

2. Add the relationship to Property

In:

app/models/property.py

add the relationship import:

from sqlalchemy.orm import Mapped, mapped_column, relationship

Then inside Property add:
Property location relationship

location_data: Mapped["PropertyLocation | None"] = relationship(
    "PropertyLocation",
    back_populates="property",
    uselist=False,
    cascade="all, delete-orphan",
)

Because this is a one-to-one relationship:

Property 1 ───────── 1 PropertyLocation

The unique=True on property_id prevents accidentally storing multiple canonical locations for the same property.
3. Make sure SQLAlchemy knows the model

Wherever your application imports models before Base.metadata.create_all(), import:

from app.models.property import Property
from app.models.location import PropertyLocation

If you already have a central model import file, add PropertyLocation there instead.

Otherwise SQLAlchemy may not know about the new table when metadata is created.
4. Create the table

Since you're currently using SQLite and appear to be using create_all(), restart the application after adding the model.

If your database initialization calls:

Base.metadata.create_all(bind=engine)

the new table should be created.

Verify:

sqlite3 your_database.db ".tables"

You should see:

properties
property_locations

If your database filename is different, use your actual SQLite path.

6. Why we're keeping POIs separate

Don't put schools, hospitals, airports, etc. into this model.

The architecture should become:

Property
    │
    └── PropertyLocation
          ├── latitude
          ├── longitude
          ├── address
          ├── district
          ├── city
          └── country

Property
    │
    └── POIs
          ├── school
          ├── hospital
          ├── airport
          ├── shopping
          ├── restaurant
          └── park

Later we'll create something like:

LocationPOI
├── id
├── name
├── category
├── latitude
├── longitude
├── address
├── city
├── country
└── source

Then we can calculate:

Property
   ↓
PropertyLocation
   ↓
POI search
   ↓
distance calculation
   ↓
Location Intelligence
   ↓
Location Score

One important detail

Your current Property already has:

location
city
country

That's fine. Those are property/source attributes.

PropertyLocation adds the normalized geospatial representation:

location = "AIDA"
city     = "Muscat"
country  = "Oman"

latitude  = ...
longitude = ...

That separation will make the later location and infrastructure engines much cleaner.

Next after this: we should add the LocationPOI model and then populate the Marriott AIDA property with coordinates before building the actual Location Intelligence scoring.

1. Step 3 — Location Data
New file

Create:

backend/app/models/location.py

Use this model:

from datetime import datetime

from sqlalchemy import DateTime, Float, Integer, String
from sqlalchemy.orm import Mapped, mapped_column

from app.db.database import Base


class Location(Base):
    __tablename__ = "locations"

    id: Mapped[int] = mapped_column(
        Integer,
        primary_key=True,
        autoincrement=True,
    )

    name: Mapped[str] = mapped_column(
        String(255),
        index=True,
    )

    location_type: Mapped[str] = mapped_column(
        String(100),
        index=True,
    )

    country: Mapped[str | None] = mapped_column(
        String(100),
        index=True,
        nullable=True,
    )

    city: Mapped[str | None] = mapped_column(
        String(150),
        index=True,
        nullable=True,
    )

    area: Mapped[str | None] = mapped_column(
        String(255),
        index=True,
        nullable=True,
    )

    address: Mapped[str | None] = mapped_column(
        String(500),
        nullable=True,
    )

    latitude: Mapped[float | None] = mapped_column(
        Float,
        nullable=True,
    )

    longitude: Mapped[float | None] = mapped_column(
        Float,
        nullable=True,
    )

    source: Mapped[str] = mapped_column(
        String(100),
        index=True,
    )

    source_url: Mapped[str | None] = mapped_column(
        String(1000),
        nullable=True,
    )

    metadata_json: Mapped[str | None] = mapped_column(
        String,
        nullable=True,
    )

    created_at: Mapped[datetime] = mapped_column(
        DateTime,
        default=datetime.utcnow,
    )

    updated_at: Mapped[datetime] = mapped_column(
        DateTime,
        default=datetime.utcnow,
        onupdate=datetime.utcnow,
    )

This gives us a generic location table that can represent:

AIDA
Dubai Marina
Downtown Dubai
Airport
Hospital
School
Metro station
Shopping mall
Beach
Road

The important field is:

location_type

because later the engine can distinguish:

school
hospital
airport
metro
mall
beach
road

backend/app/models/__init__.py

3. Location schemas

Create:

backend/app/schemas/location.py

from pydantic import BaseModel, ConfigDict


class LocationBase(BaseModel):
    name: str
    location_type: str

    country: str | None = None
    city: str | None = None
    area: str | None = None
    address: str | None = None

    latitude: float | None = None
    longitude: float | None = None

    source: str
    source_url: str | None = None


class LocationResponse(LocationBase):
    id: int

    model_config = ConfigDict(
        from_attributes=True
    )

4. Step 4 — Location Intelligence Engine

Create:

backend/app/services/location_engine.py

Start simple.

from math import radians, sin, cos, sqrt, atan2

from sqlalchemy.orm import Session

from app.models.location import Location


def haversine_distance_km(
    lat1: float,
    lon1: float,
    lat2: float,
    lon2: float,
) -> float:

    earth_radius_km = 6371.0

    d_lat = radians(lat2 - lat1)
    d_lon = radians(lon2 - lon1)

    a = (
        sin(d_lat / 2) ** 2
        +
        cos(radians(lat1))
        * cos(radians(lat2))
        * sin(d_lon / 2) ** 2
    )

    c = 2 * atan2(
        sqrt(a),
        sqrt(1 - a),
    )

    return earth_radius_km * c


def nearby_locations(
    db: Session,
    *,
    latitude: float,
    longitude: float,
    location_type: str | None = None,
    radius_km: float = 10,
    limit: int = 20,
) -> list[dict]:

    query = db.query(Location)

    if location_type:
        query = query.filter(
            Location.location_type.ilike(
                location_type
            )
        )

    locations = query.all()

    results = []

    for location in locations:

        if (
            location.latitude is None
            or location.longitude is None
        ):
            continue

        distance = haversine_distance_km(
            latitude,
            longitude,
            location.latitude,
            location.longitude,
        )

        if distance <= radius_km:
            results.append(
                {
                    "id": location.id,
                    "name": location.name,
                    "location_type": location.location_type,
                    "country": location.country,
                    "city": location.city,
                    "area": location.area,
                    "latitude": location.latitude,
                    "longitude": location.longitude,
                    "distance_km": round(
                        distance,
                        2,
                    ),
                    "source": location.source,
                    "source_url": location.source_url,
                }
            )

    results.sort(
        key=lambda item: item["distance_km"]
    )

    return results[:limit]

This is our first real Location Intelligence capability.

For example:

Property
   ↓
latitude / longitude
   ↓
Location Engine
   ↓
nearest schools
nearest hospitals
nearest metro
nearest airport
nearest malls

5. Add a location score

Still in:

app/services/location_engine.py

add:

def calculate_location_score(
    *,
    schools: int,
    hospitals: int,
    transport: int,
    lifestyle: int,
) -> float:

    score = (
        schools * 0.25
        + hospitals * 0.25
        + transport * 0.30
        + lifestyle * 0.20
    )

    return round(
        min(score, 100),
        2,
    )

But don't over-engineer this yet.

Later we will replace these simplistic counts with distance-weighted scoring.

For example:

Metro 500m      → strong
Metro 2km       → moderate
Metro 10km      → weak

6. Step 5 — Infrastructure

Don't create a completely separate infrastructure table yet.

Use the Location model.

For example:

location_type = "metro"
location_type = "airport"
location_type = "hospital"
location_type = "school"
location_type = "road"
location_type = "mall"

Then create:

backend/app/services/infrastructure_engine.py

from sqlalchemy.orm import Session

from app.services.location_engine import (
    nearby_locations,
)


INFRASTRUCTURE_TYPES = {
    "metro",
    "airport",
    "hospital",
    "school",
    "road",
}


def infrastructure_near_property(
    db: Session,
    *,
    latitude: float,
    longitude: float,
    radius_km: float = 10,
) -> dict:

    result = {}

    for location_type in INFRASTRUCTURE_TYPES:

        result[location_type] = nearby_locations(
            db,
            latitude=latitude,
            longitude=longitude,
            location_type=location_type,
            radius_km=radius_km,
            limit=10,
        )

    return result

Then add:

def calculate_infrastructure_score(
    infrastructure: dict,
) -> float:

    weights = {
        "metro": 25,
        "airport": 15,
        "hospital": 20,
        "school": 20,
        "road": 20,
    }

    score = 0

    for category, weight in weights.items():

        items = infrastructure.get(
            category,
            [],
        )

        if items:
            nearest = items[0]["distance_km"]

            if nearest <= 1:
                score += weight
            elif nearest <= 3:
                score += weight * 0.7
            elif nearest <= 5:
                score += weight * 0.4

    return round(score, 2)

Now we have:

Infrastructure
      │
      ├── Metro
      ├── Airport
      ├── Hospital
      ├── School
      └── Roads
              ↓
      Infrastructure Score
             0–100

7. Step 6 — Comparable Engine

This is where your existing Property model becomes useful.

Create:

backend/app/services/comparable_engine.py

First version should be rule-based, not LLM-based.

from sqlalchemy.orm import Session

from app.models.property import Property


def find_comparables(
    db: Session,
    property_item: Property,
    limit: int = 5,
) -> list[Property]:

    query = db.query(Property).filter(
        Property.id != property_item.id
    )

    if property_item.city:
        query = query.filter(
            Property.city.ilike(
                f"%{property_item.city}%"
            )
        )

    if property_item.property_type:
        query = query.filter(
            Property.property_type.ilike(
                f"%{property_item.property_type}%"
            )
        )

    if property_item.bedrooms is not None:
        query = query.filter(
            Property.bedrooms
            == property_item.bedrooms
        )

    return (
        query
        .limit(limit)
        .all()
    )

That gives us basic comparables:

same city
+
same property type
+
same bedrooms

8. Add price-per-square-foot

In the same file:

def price_per_area(
    property_item: Property,
) -> float | None:

    if (
        property_item.price is None
        or property_item.area is None
        or property_item.area <= 0
    ):
        return None

    return (
        property_item.price
        / property_item.area
    )

Then:

def comparable_analysis(
    property_item: Property,
    comparables: list[Property],
) -> dict:

    subject_price_per_area = price_per_area(
        property_item
    )

    comparable_values = []

    for comparable in comparables:

        value = price_per_area(
            comparable
        )

        if value is not None:
            comparable_values.append(value)

    if not comparable_values:
        return {
            "subject_price_per_area":
                subject_price_per_area,
            "comparable_count": 0,
            "average_price_per_area": None,
            "comparable_score": 0,
        }

    average_price_per_area = (
        sum(comparable_values)
        / len(comparable_values)
    )

    comparable_score = 100

    if subject_price_per_area:
        difference = abs(
            subject_price_per_area
            - average_price_per_area
        ) / average_price_per_area

        comparable_score = max(
            0,
            100 - difference * 100,
        )

    return {
        "subject_price_per_area":
            subject_price_per_area,
        "comparable_count":
            len(comparable_values),
        "average_price_per_area":
            round(
                average_price_per_area,
                2,
            ),
        "comparable_score":
            round(
                comparable_score,
                2,
            ),
    }

1. Create app/services/location_ingestion.py

Create this file:

from typing import Any

from sqlalchemy.orm import Session

from app.models.location import Location


def ingest_location(
    db: Session,
    *,
    name: str,
    location_type: str,
    country: str | None = None,
    city: str | None = None,
    area: str | None = None,
    address: str | None = None,
    latitude: float | None = None,
    longitude: float | None = None,
    source: str = "manual",
    source_url: str | None = None,
    metadata_json: str | None = None,
) -> Location:
    """
    Insert or update a location.

    A location is considered the same when its name,
    location type, city and source match.
    """

    existing = (
        db.query(Location)
        .filter(
            Location.name == name,
            Location.location_type == location_type,
            Location.city == city,
            Location.source == source,
        )
        .first()
    )

    if existing:
        existing.country = country
        existing.area = area
        existing.address = address
        existing.latitude = latitude
        existing.longitude = longitude
        existing.source_url = source_url
        existing.metadata_json = metadata_json

        db.commit()
        db.refresh(existing)

        return existing

    location = Location(
        name=name,
        location_type=location_type,
        country=country,
        city=city,
        area=area,
        address=address,
        latitude=latitude,
        longitude=longitude,
        source=source,
        source_url=source_url,
        metadata_json=metadata_json,
    )

    db.add(location)
    db.commit()
    db.refresh(location)

    return location


def ingest_locations(
    db: Session,
    locations: list[dict[str, Any]],
) -> list[Location]:
    """
    Bulk-ingest location records.
    """

    results = []

    for item in locations:
        location = ingest_location(
            db,
            name=item["name"],
            location_type=item["location_type"],
            country=item.get("country"),
            city=item.get("city"),
            area=item.get("area"),
            address=item.get("address"),
            latitude=item.get("latitude"),
            longitude=item.get("longitude"),
            source=item.get("source", "manual"),
            source_url=item.get("source_url"),
            metadata_json=item.get("metadata_json"),
        )

        results.append(location)

    return results

This gives us two useful functions:

ingest_location()

for one location, and:

ingest_locations()

for multiple locations.
2. Create a test ingestion script

Create:

app/knowledge/ingest_locations.py

Put this inside:

from app.db.database import SessionLocal

from app.services.location_ingestion import ingest_locations


locations = [
    {
        "name": "Muscat International Airport",
        "location_type": "airport",
        "country": "Oman",
        "city": "Muscat",
        "source": "manual",
    },
    {
        "name": "AIDA",
        "location_type": "development",
        "country": "Oman",
        "city": "Muscat",
        "area": "AIDA",
        "source": "darglobal",
    },
    {
        "name": "Muscat",
        "location_type": "city",
        "country": "Oman",
        "city": "Muscat",
        "source": "manual",
    },
]


def main():
    db = SessionLocal()

    try:
        results = ingest_locations(
            db,
            locations,
        )

        print(
            f"INGESTED LOCATIONS: {len(results)}"
        )

        for location in results:
            print(
                location.id,
                location.name,
                location.location_type,
                location.city,
                location.country,
            )

    finally:
        db.close()


if __name__ == "__main__":
    main()

Why this happened

Your existing database setup has:

Base = declarative_base()

and your Location class correctly uses:

class Location(Base):

So this:

Base.metadata.tables

knows about:

locations

But SQLAlchemy does not automatically create the table just because the model exists.

This command actually creates it:

Base.metadata.create_all(bind=engine)

Your existing application already does this from:

app/knowledge/ingest.py

but your new standalone location ingestion script runs independently, so it reaches:

db.query(Location)

before the table exists.
One important improvement

We should eventually avoid putting create_all() into every ingestion script. A better architecture is to have one database initialization function responsible for model registration and table creation.

But for now, the above is the smallest and safest change.

Once this succeeds, don't move to OSM yet. The next step should be the location_engine.py, where we'll connect a Property to a Location and start calculating distances/proximity and the first Location Score (0–100).

ChatGPT said:

Yes — this error tells us something important: we have already added a SQLAlchemy relationship between Property and Location, but there is currently no ForeignKey connecting the two tables.

The error:

NoForeignKeysError:
Can't find any foreign key relationships between
'properties' and 'locations'.

means SQLAlchemy sees something like:

Property.location = relationship("Location")

but cannot find:

properties.location_id → locations.id

Let's fix this properly.
1. Update app/models/property.py

Add ForeignKey and relationship to the imports.

Change:

from sqlalchemy import JSON, DateTime, Float, Integer, String, Text
from sqlalchemy.orm import Mapped, mapped_column

to:

from sqlalchemy import (
    JSON,
    DateTime,
    Float,
    ForeignKey,
    Integer,
    String,
    Text,
)

from sqlalchemy.orm import (
    Mapped,
    mapped_column,
    relationship,
)

Then add this field after location:

    location_id: Mapped[int | None] = mapped_column(
        ForeignKey("locations.id"),
        nullable=True,
        index=True,
    )

So this section becomes:

    country: Mapped[str | None] = mapped_column(
        String(100),
        index=True,
        nullable=True,
    )

    city: Mapped[str | None] = mapped_column(
        String(150),
        index=True,
        nullable=True,
    )

    location: Mapped[str | None] = mapped_column(
        String(500),
        nullable=True,
    )

    location_id: Mapped[int | None] = mapped_column(
        ForeignKey("locations.id"),
        nullable=True,
        index=True,
    )

    price: Mapped[float | None] = mapped_column(
        Float,
        nullable=True,
    )

Then near the bottom of the Property class, add:

    location_record: Mapped["Location | None"] = relationship(
        "Location",
        foreign_keys=[location_id],
    )

However, because Location isn't imported yet, use a forward reference.

At the top of property.py, add:

from typing import TYPE_CHECKING

if TYPE_CHECKING:
    from app.models.location import Location

So the imports will look approximately like:

from datetime import datetime
from typing import TYPE_CHECKING

from sqlalchemy import (
    JSON,
    DateTime,
    Float,
    ForeignKey,
    Integer,
    String,
    Text,
)

from sqlalchemy.orm import (
    Mapped,
    mapped_column,
    relationship,
)

from app.db.database import Base

if TYPE_CHECKING:
    from app.models.location import Location

2. Update app/models/location.py

Now we need the reverse relationship.

Change the imports:

from sqlalchemy.orm import Mapped, mapped_column

to:

from sqlalchemy.orm import (
    Mapped,
    mapped_column,
    relationship,
)

Add:

from typing import TYPE_CHECKING

if TYPE_CHECKING:
    from app.models.property import Property

Then at the bottom of the Location class add:

    properties: Mapped[list["Property"]] = relationship(
        "Property",
        foreign_keys="Property.location_id",
        back_populates="location_record",
    )

And update the relationship in Property to use back_populates:

    location_record: Mapped["Location | None"] = relationship(
        "Location",
        foreign_keys=[location_id],
        back_populates="properties",
    )

3. There is one more important issue: existing SQLite database

Your database already exists.

Adding:

location_id

to the SQLAlchemy model does not add the column to the existing SQLite table.

create_all() only creates missing tables. It does not perform schema migrations on existing tables.

So after making the model changes, check your database.

Run:

python3 -c '
from app.db.database import engine

with engine.connect() as conn:
    result = conn.exec_driver_sql("PRAGMA table_info(properties)")

    for row in result:
        print(row)
'

Look for:

location_id

If it isn't there, we need to add it.

For your current POC, the simplest migration is:

python3 -c '
from app.db.database import engine

with engine.begin() as conn:
    conn.exec_driver_sql(
        "ALTER TABLE properties ADD COLUMN location_id INTEGER"
    )

print("Added properties.location_id")
'

Only run that if location_id is not already present.

What is wrong

location
price

And you have two relationships:

and:

The first one is also incorrect for our design:

    "Location",
    back_populates="property",
    uselist=False,
    cascade="all, delete-orphan",

We don't want a Location to be owned by exactly one Property. For example, multiple properties can be in the same area/location.

Step 2 — Add the new columns to SQLite

This is the key part.

Base.metadata.create_all() does not modify an existing table by adding new columns. It only creates missing tables.

Since this is currently a local development SQLite database, the simplest migration is:

python3 -c '
from app.db.database import engine

with engine.begin() as conn:
    conn.exec_driver_sql(
        "ALTER TABLE properties ADD COLUMN latitude FLOAT"
    )
    conn.exec_driver_sql(
        "ALTER TABLE properties ADD COLUMN longitude FLOAT"
    )
    conn.exec_driver_sql(
        "ALTER TABLE properties ADD COLUMN location_id INTEGER"
    )

print("Migration complete")
'

Then verify:

python3 -c '
from app.db.database import engine

with engine.connect() as conn:
    result = conn.exec_driver_sql(
        "PRAGMA table_info(properties)"
    )

    for row in result:
        print(row[1])
'

You should now see at the bottom:

latitude
longitude
location_id

for p in properties:
    print(
        "PROPERTY:",
        p.project_name,
        "| location_id:",
        p.location_id,
        "| lat:",
        p.latitude,
        "| lon:",
        p.longitude,

        "LOCATION:",
        l.id,
        l.name,

ChatGPT said:

That's actually useful: latitude already exists in the SQLite table. The previous PRAGMA output was from before the schema change, or the migration partially succeeded.

Because the command uses engine.begin(), SQLite committed the successful first statement before the later failure was surfaced. So we should not rerun the whole migration blindly.
1. Check exactly which columns exist

Run:

python3 -c '
from app.db.database import engine

with engine.connect() as conn:
    result = conn.exec_driver_sql(
        "PRAGMA table_info(properties)"
    )

    for row in result:
        print(row[1], row[2])
'

You will likely see:

...
latitude FLOAT

We need to know whether longitude and location_id are also there.
2. Safely add only missing columns

Use this instead:

python3 -c '
from app.db.database import engine

required = {
    "latitude": "FLOAT",
    "longitude": "FLOAT",
    "location_id": "INTEGER",
}

with engine.begin() as conn:
    existing = {
        row[1]
        for row in conn.exec_driver_sql(
            "PRAGMA table_info(properties)"
        )
    }

    for column, column_type in required.items():
        if column in existing:
            print(f"SKIP: {column} already exists")
        else:
            conn.exec_driver_sql(
                f"ALTER TABLE properties ADD COLUMN {column} {column_type}"
            )
            print(f"ADDED: {column}")

print("Migration check complete")
'

This is safe to run even if one, two, or all three columns already exist.
3. Verify

Then:

python3 -c '
from app.db.database import engine

with engine.connect() as conn:
    result = conn.exec_driver_sql(
        "PRAGMA table_info(properties)"
    )

'

Good, this is now a Python/SQLAlchemy model registration issue, not a database-schema issue.

The error:

expression 'Location' failed to locate a name ('Location')

means Property declares:

relationship("Location")

but Location has not been imported into the SQLAlchemy model registry when the mapper is configured.

before SQLAlchemy configures the Property mapper.

4. Link the property to AIDA

Assuming AIDA exists, run:

python3 -c '
from app.db.database import SessionLocal
from app.models.location import Location
from app.models.property import Property

db = SessionLocal()

property_item = db.query(Property).filter(
    Property.project_name == "Marriott Residences Oman"
).first()

location = db.query(Location).filter(
    Location.name == "AIDA",
    Location.location_type == "development",
    Location.city == "Muscat",
).first()

if not property_item:
    raise RuntimeError("Marriott Residences Oman not found")

if not location:
    raise RuntimeError("AIDA location not found")

property_item.location_id = location.id

if location.latitude is not None:
    property_item.latitude = location.latitude

if location.longitude is not None:
    property_item.longitude = location.longitude

db.commit()

print("LINKED")
print("Property:", property_item.project_name)
print("Location:", location.name)
print("location_id:", property_item.location_id)
print("latitude:", property_item.latitude)
print("longitude:", property_item.longitude)

db.close()
'

5. Verify the relationship

Run:

python3 -c '
from app.db.database import SessionLocal
from app.models.location import Location
from app.models.property import Property

db = SessionLocal()

p = db.query(Property).filter(
    Property.project_name == "Marriott Residences Oman"
).first()

print("PROPERTY:", p.project_name)
print("LOCATION ID:", p.location_id)

if p.location_record:
    print("LOCATION:", p.location_record.name)
    print("TYPE:", p.location_record.location_type)
    print("CITY:", p.location_record.city)
    print("COUNTRY:", p.location_record.country)
    print("LAT:", p.location_record.latitude)
    print("LON:", p.location_record.longitude)
else:
    print("NO LOCATION LINK")

db.close()
'

6. Important architectural point

We're now establishing the foundation for the next engines.

Instead of putting everything directly on Property, we'll use:

Property
   │
   └── Location
         │
         ├── coordinates
         ├── airport
         ├── city
         ├── schools
         ├── hospitals
         ├── POIs
         ├── transport
         └── infrastructure

Then:

                    Property
                       │
                       ▼
                Location Engine
                       │
             ┌─────────┼─────────┐
             ▼         ▼         ▼
          Access     POIs    Location Score
             │
             ▼
     Infrastructure Engine
             │
       ┌─────┼─────┐
       ▼     ▼     ▼
     Roads Transit Airport
             │
             ▼
        Infrastructure
            Score

This is much better than making the Property model contain fields such as nearest_airport, nearest_school, hospital_distance, etc.

Step 2 — Populate coordinates

Right now:

AIDA                         lat: None  lon: None
Muscat                       lat: None  lon: None
Muscat International Airport lat: None  lon: None

The Location Engine cannot calculate:

    distance
    airport proximity
    school proximity
    hospital proximity
    infrastructure proximity
    accessibility
    location score

until coordinates exist.

For our POC, let's first manually seed known coordinates rather than introducing an external geocoding API.

Run:

python3 -c '
from app.db.database import SessionLocal
from app.models.location import Location

db = SessionLocal()

coordinates = {
    "Muscat International Airport": (23.5937, 58.2844),
    "AIDA": (23.6260, 58.4050),
    "Muscat": (23.5880, 58.3829),
}

for name, (lat, lon) in coordinates.items():
    location = db.query(Location).filter(
        Location.name == name
    ).first()

    if location:
        location.latitude = lat
        location.longitude = lon
        print(f"UPDATED: {name} -> {lat}, {lon}")

db.commit()
db.close()

print("Coordinates updated")
'

The exact AIDA coordinate is only a POC approximation; later we should replace manually seeded coordinates with authoritative/geocoded data.

   └── longitude

And Marriott Residences Oman → AIDA → coordinates is verified.
Next: Build the Location Engine

Let's do this as the next concrete implementation.
1. Create the service

Create:

touch app/services/location_engine.py

Put this in it:

from __future__ import annotations

from math import asin, cos, radians, sin, sqrt
from typing import Any

from sqlalchemy.orm import Session

from app.models.location import Location
from app.models.property import Property


EARTH_RADIUS_KM = 6371.0


def haversine_distance_km(
    latitude_1: float,
    longitude_1: float,
    latitude_2: float,
    longitude_2: float,
) -> float:
    """
    Calculate the great-circle distance between two
    geographic coordinates in kilometers.
    """

    lat1 = radians(latitude_1)
    lon1 = radians(longitude_1)

    lat2 = radians(latitude_2)
    lon2 = radians(longitude_2)

    delta_lat = lat2 - lat1
    delta_lon = lon2 - lon1

    a = (
        sin(delta_lat / 2) ** 2
        + cos(lat1)
        * cos(lat2)
        * sin(delta_lon / 2) ** 2
    )

    c = 2 * asin(sqrt(a))

    return EARTH_RADIUS_KM * c


def get_property_location(
    db: Session,
    property_id: int,
) -> Location | None:
    """
    Resolve the Location associated with a property.
    """

    property_item = (
        db.query(Property)
        .filter(Property.id == property_id)
        .first()
    )

    if not property_item:
        return None

    if not property_item.location_id:
        return None

    return (
        db.query(Location)
        .filter(Location.id == property_item.location_id)
        .first()
    )


def calculate_distance_to_location(
    property_location: Location,
    target_location: Location,
) -> float | None:
    """
    Calculate distance between two Location records.
    """

    if (
        property_location.latitude is None
        or property_location.longitude is None
        or target_location.latitude is None
        or target_location.longitude is None
    ):
        return None

    return haversine_distance_km(
        property_location.latitude,
        property_location.longitude,
        target_location.latitude,
        target_location.longitude,
    )


def find_nearby_locations(
    db: Session,
    property_location: Location,
    *,
    location_type: str | None = None,
    radius_km: float = 50.0,
    limit: int = 10,
) -> list[dict[str, Any]]:
    """
    Find nearby Location records within a radius.
    """

    if (
        property_location.latitude is None
        or property_location.longitude is None
    ):
        return []

    query = db.query(Location).filter(
        Location.id != property_location.id,
        Location.latitude.is_not(None),
        Location.longitude.is_not(None),
    )

    if location_type:
        query = query.filter(
            Location.location_type == location_type
        )

    locations = query.all()

    results = []

    for location in locations:
        distance = calculate_distance_to_location(
            property_location,
            location,
        )

        if distance is None:
            continue

        if distance > radius_km:
            continue

        results.append(
            {
                "id": location.id,
                "name": location.name,
                "location_type": location.location_type,
                "country": location.country,
                "city": location.city,
                "area": location.area,
                "latitude": location.latitude,
                "longitude": location.longitude,
                "distance_km": round(distance, 2),
                "source": location.source,
                "source_url": location.source_url,
            }
        )

    results.sort(
        key=lambda item: item["distance_km"]
    )

    return results[:limit]


def analyze_property_location(
    db: Session,
    property_id: int,
) -> dict[str, Any]:
    """
    Produce the initial location intelligence result
    for a property.
    """

    location = get_property_location(
        db,
        property_id,
    )

    if not location:
        return {
            "property_id": property_id,
            "location_found": False,
            "location_score": None,
            "evidence": [],
            "confidence": 0.0,
        }

    nearby_airports = find_nearby_locations(
        db,
        location,
        location_type="airport",
        radius_km=100,
        limit=5,
    )

    nearby_developments = find_nearby_locations(
        db,
        location,
        location_type="development",
        radius_km=50,
        limit=5,
    )

    nearby_cities = find_nearby_locations(
        db,
        location,
        location_type="city",
        radius_km=100,
        limit=5,
    )

    evidence = []

    if nearby_airports:
        nearest_airport = nearby_airports[0]

        evidence.append(
            {
                "type": "airport_proximity",
                "name": nearest_airport["name"],
                "distance_km": nearest_airport["distance_km"],
                "source": nearest_airport["source"],
                "source_url": nearest_airport["source_url"],
            }
        )

    if nearby_cities:
        nearest_city = nearby_cities[0]

        evidence.append(
            {
                "type": "city_proximity",
                "name": nearest_city["name"],
                "distance_km": nearest_city["distance_km"],
                "source": nearest_city["source"],
                "source_url": nearest_city["source_url"],
            }
        )

    # Initial POC score.
    #
    # This is intentionally simple. We will replace this
    # with a proper weighted scoring model after adding
    # infrastructure and POI intelligence.
    location_score = 50.0

    if nearby_airports:
        airport_distance = nearby_airports[0]["distance_km"]

        if airport_distance <= 10:
            location_score += 20
        elif airport_distance <= 25:
            location_score += 15
        elif airport_distance <= 50:
            location_score += 10

    location_score = min(
        100.0,
        location_score,
    )

    return {
        "property_id": property_id,
        "location_found": True,
        "location": {
            "id": location.id,
            "name": location.name,
            "location_type": location.location_type,
            "country": location.country,
            "city": location.city,
            "area": location.area,
            "latitude": location.latitude,
            "longitude": location.longitude,
        },
        "nearby_airports": nearby_airports,
        "nearby_developments": nearby_developments,
        "nearby_cities": nearby_cities,
        "location_score": round(
            location_score,
            2,
        ),
        "evidence": evidence,
        "confidence": 0.80,
    }

One important architectural point

Don't worry about the score being sophisticated yet.

We're deliberately building the engines in layers:

                    LOCATION ENGINE
                         │
          ┌──────────────┼──────────────┐
          ▼              ▼              ▼
     Coordinates     Distances       Nearby POIs
          │              │              │
          └──────────────┼──────────────┘
                         ▼
                 Accessibility
                         │
                         ▼
                  Location Score

Then we'll make the score meaningful by adding the Infrastructure Engine.

Step 1 — Create the infrastructure model

Create:

touch app/models/infrastructure.py

Put this in:

from datetime import datetime

from sqlalchemy import DateTime, Float, Integer, String, Text
from sqlalchemy.orm import Mapped, mapped_column

from app.db.database import Base


class Infrastructure(Base):
    __tablename__ = "infrastructure"

    id: Mapped[int] = mapped_column(
        Integer,
        primary_key=True,
        autoincrement=True,
    )

    name: Mapped[str] = mapped_column(
        String(255),
        index=True,
    )

    infrastructure_type: Mapped[str] = mapped_column(
        String(100),
        index=True,
    )

    country: Mapped[str | None] = mapped_column(
        String(100),
        index=True,
        nullable=True,
    )

    city: Mapped[str | None] = mapped_column(
        String(150),
        index=True,
        nullable=True,
    )

    area: Mapped[str | None] = mapped_column(
        String(255),
        nullable=True,
    )

    address: Mapped[str | None] = mapped_column(
        String(500),
        nullable=True,
    )

    latitude: Mapped[float | None] = mapped_column(
        Float,
        nullable=True,
    )

    longitude: Mapped[float | None] = mapped_column(
        Float,
        nullable=True,
    )

    importance: Mapped[float | None] = mapped_column(
        Float,
        nullable=True,
    )

    description: Mapped[str | None] = mapped_column(
        Text,
        nullable=True,
    )

    source: Mapped[str] = mapped_column(
        String(100),
        index=True,
    )

    source_url: Mapped[str | None] = mapped_column(
        String(1000),
        nullable=True,
    )

    created_at: Mapped[datetime] = mapped_column(
        DateTime,
        default=datetime.utcnow,
    )

    updated_at: Mapped[datetime] = mapped_column(
        DateTime,
        default=datetime.utcnow,
        onupdate=datetime.utcnow,
    )

locations

infrastructure

Why a separate Infrastructure table?

Don't put hospitals, airports, roads, schools, etc. directly into Location.

Location represents a property's geographic/master location.

Infrastructure represents things around that location.

That distinction will become important when we calculate:

Airport distance
Hospital distance
School distance
Road accessibility
Shopping proximity
Transit proximity

and eventually:

Infrastructure Score = weighted combination

        "area": "Mutrah",

        "source": "manual",

def ingest_infrastructure(
    db: Session,
) -> int:
    """
    Insert infrastructure records if they do not already exist.
    """

    inserted = 0

    for item in INFRASTRUCTURE_DATA:
        existing = (
            db.query(Infrastructure)
            .filter(
                Infrastructure.name == item["name"],
                Infrastructure.infrastructure_type
                == item["infrastructure_type"],
                Infrastructure.city == item["city"],
            )
            .first()
        )

        if existing:
            continue

        infrastructure = Infrastructure(
            **item,
        )

        db.add(infrastructure)
        inserted += 1

    db.commit()

    return inserted

        print(
            f"INGESTED INFRASTRUCTURE: {inserted}"
        )

    finally:
        db.close()


if __name__ == "__main__":
    main()
